#6867. Trump administration puts end to mandated boost in Head Start pay

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

In a new final rule, HHS is rolling back a Biden-era effort to keep salaries competitive and reduce high staff turnover in the early education program.

The Trump administration is ending efforts to boost pay and benefits for Head Start employees, according to a final rule from the U.S. Department of Health and Human Services scheduled to be published Monday. Those efforts would have seen most Head Start teachers earn an additional $10,000 in annual salaries to align with pay for other public preschool educators.

The final rule reverses a 2024 Biden-era rule that supporters said would have helped Head Start early childhood education programs recruit and retain qualified staff and provide consistent quality programming for children and families. The 2024 rule for increased wages and benefits would have been fully implemented in 2031.

Instead, the final rule set to publish on Monday removes those requirements, calling the directive "costly and overly prescriptive for Head Start programs and staff." HHS' Administration for Children and Families, which administers the Head Start program, said its final rule will yield more than $2 billion in cost savings as it restores "greater flexibility to local Head Start programs."

Removing the wage and benefit requirements will prevent the elimination of 106,000 Head Start classroom slots.

Average Head Start teacher salaries range from $26,964 in Mississippi to $79,853 in the District of Columbia, according to a 2024 Urban Institute brief.

The change is just one part of a broader, ongoing effort by the Trump administration to overhaul the Head Start program.

The National Head Start Association, a nonprofit organization that represents Head Start families, providers and educators, said in a Friday statement that underfunding is straining the program's ability to pay staff competitively while maintaining student enrollment levels.

NHSA also said that removing federal wage and benefit requirements "does not change competitive pressures or eliminate the staffing shortages that continue to limit some programs' ability to open classrooms and serve more eligible children and families."

NHSA Executive Director Yasmina Vinci said in a statement, "A strong Head Start program depends on a stable, qualified workforce, and programs must have the resources necessary to recruit, retain, and support the professionals who serve children and families every day."

In a statement responding to the final rule, the National Association for the Education of Young Children said, "HHS frames this as a choice between paying educators and serving children, but a funded slot without a qualified teacher serves no one."

The final rule is to take effect 60 days after publication.

#6862. Unraveling All the Details Behind Wicker’s Wicker

We asked the artists behind Alexander Skarsgård’s woven-to-order suit how they built it—and kept his appeal intact.

“It will take her another lifetime to understand wicker,” Marella Agnelli reportedly said of fellow socialite Mercedes Bass’s apartment. With Alexander Skarsgård’s help, some of us are getting there a little faster.

In Wicker, the romantic comedy based on Ursula Wills’s short story “The Wicker Husband,” Olivia Colman plays a fisherwoman who commissions a husband from the local basketmaker (Peter Dinklage). Skarsgård plays her woven-to-order spouse, whose resemblance to patio furniture does surprisingly little to diminish his sex appeal. Directed by Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer, the film comes out in theaters today.

Behind the wicker transformation is Wētā Workshop, the New Zealand design and effects company whose credits include The Lord of the Rings and Avatar. Its artists make the costumes, props, and creatures that give imaginary worlds something tangible—and, in this case, marriageable.

For prosthetic makeup designer Joe Dunckley and lead makeup effects artist Sarah Rubano, the assignment was to turn Skarsgård into a convincing wicker husband without losing the expressions that make him recognizable. The team spent 18 months developing and manufacturing his suit and facial prosthetics, translating his features into a weave, right down to his furrowed brows.

Naturally, we had questions. How do you make wicker sexy? What happens to the eyelashes at lunch? And could anyone involved look at a household basket the same way again? Below, Dunckley and Rubano explain the work behind the wicker.

Before we get to the cheekbones, how did this assignment arrive?

Dunckley: We were approached by Eleanor Wilson and Alex Huston Fischer about 18 months out from filming. Initially, they were after a piece of concept art—a beautiful piece that they could take to potential cast members. This was part of their development process, so they had a tool for helping show their vision for the project.

Pretty quickly after that came the question: How are you going to make this wicker husband character? So much of the film’s success hinges on the success of that character. They always wanted to make the wicker husband a physical prosthetic character. That led us into a process of research and development. It was an 18-month period of R&D and manufacture to get to the final product.

A husband made to order. What, exactly, was in the order?

Dunckley: About 95% of the prosthetic and fabricated suit is made out of a 3D-printed material. Most prosthetic materials are manufactured to imitate human skin, but the wicker husband is built of wicker, not flesh and bones, so we knew we needed something different.

Sometimes we found the limits of existing programs and had to write our own code for weaving. Weaving is such complex geometry that it was very challenging for sculptors to weave in 3D.

The prosthetic appliances were then printed directly, without needing to go through the mold-making process.

Rubano: On [Skarsgård’s] body, we had about 50 individual pieces stitched and anchored onto thin spandex or mesh. The face, including the wig, was about 10 individual pieces. We also used foam latex for the lips, eye bags, and underlip and chin piece.

Did anyone get to weave an actual basket, or was this strictly a digital operation?

Dunckley: Absolutely. We had a team of weavers who had just come off Avatar, where they’d been weaving costumes for the Na’vi. Our design team would block out the shapes that we liked, and our lead weaver would do a physical weave sample using flax, giving us those shapes.

We’d print, do a makeup test, and learn what we learned: This one’s too loose. This one’s too tight and it binds. We’ll need more movement here. Slowly, we developed what you ended up seeing in the film.

I had a crush on the wicker husband. How do you make wicker handsome?

Dunckley: One of our main objectives was to make sure Alexander shines through the makeup. He’s a handsome man, and he has an expressive quality that’s really unique and recognizable.

We wanted to get those qualities into the weave. It was a matter of deconstructing what it is about those expressions that are endearing, that are heart-melting, and then reconstructing them in a weave pattern and enabling that movement for him through the prosthetic.

How long did it take for Alexander Skarsgård to become the wicker husband every day of filming?

Rubano: We got the whole morning process down to an hour and a half. That was the brief at the beginning, because he was working every day. It was an ambitious shooting schedule, so all the build techniques supported being able to expedite it.

He would come in, and we would put on a partial fabricated undersuit. He would come to the makeup chair, then go back and get more of the body suit on. Then we got into gluing the appliances to his face, the eyelashes, the wig, the styling, the makeup around the eyes—all the finishing touches.

And at lunch? Surely even a wicker husband gets a break.

Rubano: About halfway through, once we were really confident with our processes, we started giving him a bit of relief at lunchtime. I would take off the eyelashes because they’re quite robust, and he’s not used to wearing fake eyelashes. We would wrap up his beard and put a little bib over it so he could eat and be comfortable. We’d take off the gloves—just all the things we could do to give him some reprieve. After lunch, we’d build him up 100% again. The cleanup at the end of the day was about half an hour. The real challenge was keeping him cool. He runs hot, so we had fans and ice packs.

How do you feel about all the online chatter about “sexy wicker”?

Dunckley: We never would have expected this kind of level, to be honest. It’s been quite a ride to watch the enthusiasm, all the excitement, all the memes. We sat around watching memes for hours and laughing our heads off.

Rubano: We’ve built so many different types of characters for so many different types of films over 30-plus years. Sometimes they strike a chord as hoped, sometimes they don’t. Sometimes the chord has been struck louder than you could ever have expected. This is an example of that.

Has this changed how you look at the wicker in your own home?

Rubano: I have this tiny little wicker basket, and it’s not necessarily sexy, but I do love it. I look at it with love and adoration. It’s just this tiny little basket I put all my jewelry bits and pieces in. But, of course, the hottest basket of all is Alexander Skarsgård. Definitely.

#6858. Swimply: Regulatory Affairs Manager

Swimply is the marketplace that lets homeowners rent out their private pools, hot tubs, courts and backyards by the hour. Thousands of hosts across the United States and Canada use Swimply to share space they already own with families and small groups in their community.

Because hourly backyard rentals are new, most local codes were written before they existed. Towns, counties, health departments and homeowners associations regularly try to fit Swimply into rules written for hotels, public pools, home businesses or overnight short-term rentals. We are building a dedicated regulatory function to manage those matters at scale and to move the rules in our favor, state by state.

You will own the day-to-day operation of the program and work directly with our General Counsel, our CEO, our Head of Operations and the outside lobbyists and local counsel we retain.

The Regulatory Affairs Manager runs Swimply's regulatory program end to end. Half of the job is operational: every notice a host or the company receives is logged, answered and tracked to a close. The other half is strategic: finding the bills, partners and officials that let us fix the rules for a whole state at once instead of town by town.

#6852. New Industry Alliance to Work Toward Common Security Model for AI Agents

Okta has announced a coalition bringing together major cloud, security, data and application vendors to create a shared architecture for securing AI agents across enterprise environments.

The 12 founding members of the new Blueprint Alliance are AWS, CrowdStrike, Databricks, Docker, Google Cloud, Lovable, Okta, Proofpoint, Salesforce, ServiceNow, Wiz and Zscaler. GE Appliances and World Central Kitchen are participating as strategic advisors.

The effort expands an earlier Okta framework into an open, multi-vendor reference architecture intended to address a basic problem with enterprise AI agents: An agent can be created in one system, authenticated through another, access tools and data from several others, and be monitored by yet another security platform.

The Alliance is focused less on how agents are built and more on how organizations can govern them consistently across vendor boundaries.

The Blueprint Alliance architecture organizes agent security around four questions: Where are my agents? What can they do? What are they doing? How do I respond?

Those questions translate into several practical areas:

The reference architecture also covers development-related systems. Its whitepaper includes agent frameworks, code repositories, CI/CD environments, code assistants, tool registries, Model Context Protocol servers, and command-line tools among the components that may need to be discovered or governed.

That makes the initiative relevant to development teams deploying agents that interact with internal APIs, MCP tools, databases, SaaS applications, or other agents. The architecture calls for those connections and permissions to remain visible and controllable as agents move from development into production.

A major part of the Alliance's work will be testing how security information and controls can move between products from different vendors.

The initial architecture references several existing standards and protocols, including MCP for agent-to-tool interactions, the Open Cybersecurity Schema Framework for security telemetry, and the Shared Signals Framework and Continuous Access Evaluation Profile for exchanging risk information.

The goal is for one part of an enterprise stack to be able to detect a risk condition and pass that information to another control point that can act on it. Depending on the implementation, that could include revoking access or containing an agent whose behavior has changed.

The Alliance says members will build and test reference integrations and publish results as interoperability work progresses. Specific cross-vendor implementations and performance results were not documented in the launch announcement.

Founding member Lovable also published a developer-oriented explanation of its participation, describing organizations that may have internally built agents, agents imported through SaaS platforms and locally running agents that all need to be inventoried and governed under a common model.

The Alliance's whitepaper recommends that organizations begin with foundational capabilities such as unified logging, telemetry, and agent discovery rather than attempting to deploy every control at once.

The architecture is available for community use, and the Alliance says it will continue evolving as members test integrations and additional participants become involved. Timelines for individual vendor integrations have not been published.

For more information, check out the Okta announcement here.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

#6850. AI Platform Behind 12,000 E-mail Breaches Dismantled

The platform moved quickly. Since launching in February, EvilTokens had been tied to more than 12,000 compromised inboxes across over 10,000 organizations worldwide, according to Microsoft. Its victims included businesses and institutions in financial services, healthcare, construction, real estate, wholesale distribution, and higher education.

Microsoft and its partners seized 50 websites used to operate EvilTokens and disabled more than 150 additional domains supporting the service. The Metropolitan Police Service in the United Kingdom also arrested two men on Sept. 11 in connection with the alleged operation.

At the center of EvilTokens was an AI chatbot designed specifically for cybercrime. Once attackers gained access to an inbox, the chatbot could analyze messages, map relationships, identify employees involved in payments, and recommend ways to impersonate trusted contacts.

Microsoft said the platform helped criminals determine "who to target, who to impersonate," and how to get the most money from a compromised organization.

Business e-mail compromise has traditionally required attackers to manually comb through messages, learn how an organization handles payments, and find the right person to impersonate. However, EvilTokens simplified the entire process by automating much of that work.

The tools could quickly sort through a stolen inbox, summarize or translate messages, find invoices and wire-transfer discussions, and identify the people who moved money. EvilTokens could then draft messages that sounded like they came from the victim. It packaged all of this with mailbox access, phishing tools, management dashboards, and even customer support.

The service was sold through Telegram for a $1,500 startup fee, followed by a $500 monthly subscription. Microsoft's complaint also described a bulk e-mail tool that cost $600 a month and a more advanced e-mail delivery platform priced at $1,000 a month.

To break into a target, EvilTokens relied on device-code phishing. Victims were sent to a real Microsoft sign-in page and persuaded to enter an authentication code. The login process looked legitimate because it was, but the code quietly authorized a session controlled by the attacker.

That also made cleanup more complicated. The attackers stole OAuth tokens and authenticated sessions instead of passwords, so a password reset alone might not remove their access. Administrators also needed to revoke the stolen tokens and active sessions.

According to Microsoft's complaint,  EvilTokens used fake Microsoft 365 security notices and other branded templates to direct users into the device authentication flow. Once inside, attackers could use Microsoft Graph and other APIs to search and monitor a mailbox.

The company also found that AI played a role in creating EvilTokens itself. Investigators said much of the platform appeared to have been "vibe coded," with its developers using AI to quickly build and expand the service. EvilTokens reportedly drew on multiple AI models, including improperly integrated services from Groq and OpenAI.

The court authorized Microsoft to take control of or disable domains connected to the operation. Microsoft worked with Cloudflare, Coinbase, OpenAI, Railway, SpyCloud, the Shadowserver Foundation, and TRM Labs on the disruption. Health-ISAC joined the case because healthcare organizations were among the targets.

This marks the Microsoft Digital Crimes Unit's 40th court-authorized disruption and its first targeting an end-to-end, AI-enabled cybercrime service.

EvilTokens' infrastructure may be offline, but the model behind it is unlikely to disappear. The operation showed how AI can turn a stolen inbox into an immediately useful fraud playbook, allowing less-experienced criminals to launch attacks that once required considerable time and skill.

For more information, go to the Microsoft blog post.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6845. Redesigned Microsoft Copilot Will Build Apps and Work on Its Own

Micorosft has announced that it's redesigning Copilot, the company's all-encompassing AI assistant, so users can hand off complex tasks, build tools by describing what they need, and assign work to an agent that keeps going after they log off.

The new Copilot experience has three main parts: Home, Code, and Autopilot. It also brings Word, Excel, and PowerPoint into the Copilot app. For users, the pitch is simple: Start a task in one place and stay there as it grows from a quick question into a document, an app, or a longer-running project. Most of the new capabilities are still rolling out or in preview.

Home puts Chat and Copilot Cowork together. Chat remains the place for quick answers, research, and drafts. Cowork handles work a user wants to delegate, such as preparing a briefing or pulling together a response to a request for proposals. Microsoft said Copilot eventually will route requests to Chat, Cowork, or Code on its own.

Office in Copilot will allow users to create a Word document, Excel workbook, or PowerPoint deck without leaving that experience. The files remain editable in the regular Office apps, and colleagues can work on them at the same time. Someone building a presentation, for example, could draw on a workbook, draft the slides, and keep refining both files from within Copilot.

Code is aimed at people who need a tool but might never open a development environment. A user can describe a tracker, dashboard, app, or workflow in plain language and have Copilot build it. Microsoft calls these "small, purpose-built solutions." The feature uses the same underlying technology as GitHub Copilot and runs in a sandboxed environment. A separate Copilot Managed Runtime, now in preview, lets organizations host and share the resulting apps within Microsoft 365.

Then there is Autopilot, previously known as Scout. It is designed for work that does not end with a single response: following a discussion, checking in with stakeholders, or keeping a project moving over several days. "Give it a name, a role, and a goal," Microsoft said. Autopilot runs in the cloud and has its own identity, memory, and workspace in the customer's tenant. Microsoft said its actions are subject to permissions, auditing, and organizational controls.

Those longer-running tasks also change how customers pay for Copilot. In a separate pricing post, Microsoft said everyday work in Chat and Microsoft 365 apps will remain covered by a per-user subscription. Cowork, Code, and Autopilot will be billed separately through usage-based Copilot Credits. A Copilot user subscription will still be needed to access those features.

For enterprise customers, the features that charge by usage will stay off until an admin sets up a spending policy in the Microsoft 365 admin center. Microsoft said there will be no charges before then. Admins can set budgets for the whole organization or for specific groups, limit how much individual users can spend, and get alerts when costs approach a set amount. Reports will show who is using the features and which tasks are driving costs.

Microsoft is bringing Code and Copilot Managed Runtime under those cost controls alongside Cowork. Copilot Studio agents are expected to follow in October. Before turning the features on for more employees, IT departments will need to decide who can use them, how much they can spend and which tasks are worth the extra cost.

Home and Code will begin rolling out through Microsoft's Frontier early access program in the coming weeks. Code's Frontier rollout starts at the end of September, when Autopilot also expands to private preview.

For more information, visit the Microsoft site.

Chris Paoli (@ChrisPaoli5) is the associate editor for Converge360.

#6843. Time to Break Down That Major Twist at the End of ‘Campus Drivers’ and How It Differs From the Books

The Prime Video series takes a major turn from C.S. Quill’s series.

If—after watching the sixth episode of Campus Drivers—you wondered whether there were still more episodes to come, I’ve got bad news: That really was the finale. Season 1 of the French series from Prime Video ends with a twist that leaves plenty of questions, especially about Lane and Lois’s future. It’s a choice that even surprised those familiar with the book series by C.S. Quill, because the TV ending takes a different direction from the novel’s.

But what exactly happens in the final episode? And, above all, why does Lane decide to leave for Los Angeles right when his relationship with Lois finally seemed destined to take off? Let’s get into it.

In the season 1 finale, we discover that Lane leaves with his mother to move to L.A. and attend film school—a decision that, at first glance, seems to come out of nowhere, especially considering the bond that has formed with Lois over the course of the episodes. Behind this choice, however, there is a secret motivation: Lane’s mother does not approve of the path her son wants to take and believes his career choice is not the right one, but she accepts it on the condition that he leave Bellerose and end his relationship with Lois; otherwise, she is prepared to interfere with Lois’s scholarship, since she considers her an unsuitable partner for Lane.

At that point, Lane is faced with an impossible decision: stay beside the person he is falling in love with, or sacrifice his own happiness to allow her to continue studying medicine at a prestigious university.

Lane chooses the second option. He agrees to leave with his mother, thereby giving up the healthiest relationship of his life just when the two of them were finally beginning to truly grow closer.

The season’s final scene makes the separation even more concrete. In fact, we see movers carrying the furniture out of Lane’s apartment: an image that confirms his departure and the fact that the move to the United States is not just a threat or a possibility, but a decision that has now been made.

What’s more, Lois does not know the real reason for Lane’s departure. She does not know that he agreed to leave only to protect her future and allow her to continue her studies. In her eyes, that separation therefore risks seeming like a personal choice, a renunciation of their story just when it could have begun. At this point, the series departs considerably from the novel: the TV ending changes the protagonists’ journey and chooses to interrupt their story at a particularly delicate point, leaving the most important issue unresolved: the two protagonists did not get their romantic happy ending.

The ending seems designed precisely to leave the door open to a possible sequel (with the two of them still as major protagonists) and to heighten the drama—although for now, an official renewal has yet to be announced. Here’s hoping we will see Lane and Lois again in a second season!

#6841. AI Leaders Warn of Risks at United Nations Security Council Briefing

The heads of OpenAI, Anthropic, and Hugging Face recently joined AI researcher Yoshua Bengio at the United Nations headquarters in New York on September 23 to warn the UN Security Council about the security risks of increasingly capable AI.

They did not offer a single plan for managing those risks. Bengio called for licensing and independent safety reviews. OpenAI's Sam Altman proposed common standards that countries could adopt through their own laws. Anthropic's Dario Amodei urged AI developers to slow down when safety work cannot keep pace. Hugging Face's Clément Delangue warned that limiting access to capable systems could leave defenders at a disadvantage.

France, which held the Security Council's rotating presidency, convened the meeting on AI and international security. The discussion exposed a difficult question for governments: how to oversee technology that crosses borders when countries disagree about who should set the rules.

Bengio, co-chair of the UN's Independent International Scientific Panel on AI, challenged the idea that competition forces companies to keep building more powerful systems. "The race is not a law of nature: it is the product of choices," he said in his published remarks. "They've told us they would slow down if they could. They can."

He called on developers to show independent experts that frontier systems are safe to train and deploy. He also proposed incident reporting, licensing, and liability insurance. Bengio identified catastrophic misuse, concentrated power, and the loss of human control as risks that require international cooperation.

Altman acknowledged the danger of moving too quickly. "We have unilaterally slowed down in the past. We will do so in the future," he said, according to remarks published by OpenAI. Decisions about AI's direction "cannot be made by labs in San Francisco alone," he added.

Altman proposed complementary national and international standards to measure frontier AI capabilities, assess risks, and test safeguards. He said countries should be able to compare evidence and verify commitments. He also called for faster reporting of serious incidents and secure channels for sharing emerging threats. Such standards should accommodate both open and closed models without favoring established companies, he said.

Amodei made a more direct commitment about the pace of Anthropic's releases: "We will slow down as much as necessary," he told the council. He identified two principal concerns: the misuse of AI to develop biological weapons and the possibility that advanced systems could outstrip developers' ability to control them.

The Anthropic chief proposed placing outside evaluators inside AI companies with access comparable to employees. He suggested that governments begin with a narrow agreement against using AI to make biological weapons, then develop ways to evaluate models and verify commitments across borders. He also called for common testing standards and a system for notifying other countries of significant AI incidents.

Delangue drew on a July incident in which Hugging Face disclosed an AI agent cyberattack. He called for stronger monitoring and disclosure, but argued that safety also depends on giving defenders access to capable tools. The central danger, he said, was "asymmetry of powerful AI" between attackers and defenders, large companies and everyone else, and wealthy countries and the rest of the world. His team used an open-source model while responding to the attack, he said.

The U.S. government opposed a broader international regulatory role. White House science adviser Michael Kratsios told the council that countries should develop their own ability to use and govern the technology. In the U.S. statement, he rejected new global governance structures and used the phrase "superintelligence, formerly known as AI," adopting President Donald Trump's terminology from his General Assembly speech the previous day.

The four AI briefers differed on how far oversight should go and how widely capable systems should be available. They agreed on a narrower point: Governments need better ways to understand what advanced AI systems can do and to detect when safeguards fail.

#6838. Here’s How Much the Mirrorball Trophy Is Worth on ‘Dancing With the Stars’

We’re just a few weeks out from the Dancing With the Stars season 35 finale (it goes down on November 24), which means one couple is about to walk away with the Len Goodman Mirrorball Trophy. You know, the giant disco ball that everyone hoists above their heads after winning?

And if you’re wondering how much it’s worth, the answer is PRICELESS, obviously. But also, kind of a lot.

The mirrorball itself is incredibly cheap. Production designer James Yarnell told People in 2017, “No matter how much we put into the trophy, how much we spend, it always has a $10 mirrorball on top.” The show does throw down for the base, however. Winner Kaitlyn Bristowe told Trading Secrets that, all together, the trophy is worth around $10,000.

Apparently Hollywood 3D Printing—who uses “3D printing technologies combined with advanced finishing capabilities such as detailed painting, electroplating, and vacuum plating”—is responsible for making the Mirrorball Trophies, which they confirmed on Instagram in 2023.

Meanwhile, this glitzy statue has come a long way since the early days of Dancing With the Stars. Yarnell said he made the original Mirrorball Trophy (which Kelly Monaco won) from a brass lamp, a wooden base, and some tape. And it stayed that way for four full seasons! Per Yarnell, “They stopped making the lamp at Lamps Plus, so we’d run out of options and had to start casting it and put a heavy acrylic base with a brass stem.”

As for whether or not celebs and pros keep their trophies, it presumably depends on the person. But Derek Hough is so attached to his collection that he literally travels with them on tour and puts them in the lobby for fans to enjoy.

#6835. Mark Rober's Class CrunchLabs Courses Now Available for Free on YouTube

Class CrunchLabs, the suite of free curriculum resources from NASA engineer turned YouTube science communicator Mark Rober, has released its first two courses on YouTube.

Designed in partnership with the National Science Teaching Association, the standards-aligned "Carnival of Collisions: Contact Forces" and "Mission MagLev: Electric & Magentic Forces" courses offer free video lessons, guided activities, and supplemental teaching resources for grades 6-8.

Class CrunchLabs was launched last year to offer free hands-on science curriculum resources including teaching guides, classroom slides, student readings and handouts, assessments, and instructional videos featuring Rober alongside "Science Bob" Pflugfelder, celebrity guests, and student co-hosts. The initiative was backed by a $7.2 million grant to create the CrunchLabs.org Foundation, a nonprofit "dedicated to revolutionizing science education in a way that promotes critical thinking and inspires lasting curiosity and confidence in STEM," according to a news announcement at the time.

The organization plans to roll out additional courses on YouTube over time, with the ultimate goal of posting more than 1,000 science videos in 34 languages.

Check out the Class CrunchLabs YouTube channel here.

#6833. The ‘Campus Drivers’ Finale May Have Revealed Who Season 2 Will Be About, and It’s a Change From the Books

Lois and Lane’s unfinished story could signal a major shift.

The season 1 finale of Campus Drivers left viewers with several questions, one of them being: Who will the protagonists of the next season be? Prime Video’s French-language series is based on the book series by C.S. Quill, but it has already demonstrated that it doesn’t necessarily intend to follow the novels’ path to the letter. And the very way Lane and Lois’s story ends could foreshadow an important narrative choice for the future of the adaptation.

As a reminder: In the series finale, Lane decides to leave for Los Angeles with his mother in order to allow Lois to keep her scholarship. Their story is cut short just when it seemed destined to turn into something more, leaving several questions open that could become central in a second season.

However, season 1 does also end with a twist involving Donovan. Without giving too much away about what happens in the second book, Crash Course, which is centered on Donovan, the ending sees (spoiler!) Donovan’s dad collapse during a basketball practice. Still, Lois and Lane remain at the center of the narrative with a story still entirely unresolved…and this choice may not be accidental.

The choice to keep Lane and Lois at the center of the story could suggest that Campus Drivers will not necessarily follow the narrative model of Off Campus. In the series based on Elle Kennedy’s novels, each season is designed to mainly tell the story of a new couple, giving space to different protagonists within the same narrative universe.

For Campus Drivers (despite the series having the same structure), the direction could be different: keeping Lane and Lois as central figures in the coming seasons as well, gradually developing the love stories of the other characters and giving them more and more space. Based on the season 1 ending, it’s possible that it will instead use an increasingly ensemble structure, in which the protagonists’ stories intertwine and become richer episode after episode, without necessarily making way for a new main couple each season.

This does not rule out the possibility that, further down the line, other characters could take on an even more important role. As early as the third season, for example, there could be room to explore the secondary love stories in greater depth. For now, however, these are only hypotheses; Prime Video has not yet confirmed what the structure of any future chapters will be.

If the series is renewed, it is reasonable to expect Lane and Lois to maintain a central role. The first season finale left their relationship hanging, and it would be difficult to imagine a follow-up that does not pick up precisely from what happened between them—especially because six episodes felt like too few to fully develop a dynamic between the leads.

The choice not to follow the structure of the novels could also be a way to differentiate Campus Drivers from Off Campus, especially since both series share the same platform and target the same audience of fans.

At the moment, a second season has not yet been officially confirmed. However, considering the attention the series is receiving and the narrative potential left open by the finale, it would not be surprising to see new updates arrive soon.

#6832. OpenAI Getting Rid of Custom GPTs and Moving to Plugin Architecture

OpenAI has announced it will retire Custom GPTs and move the specialized workflows built around them to a new plugin architecture spanning ChatGPT and its Codex coding agent.

Custom GPT retirement is currently scheduled for Dec. 11, 2026, although the company says timing and migration availability can vary by account and workspace.

For affected Enterprise workspaces, OpenAI's current migration schedule targets Oct. 26 for ending creation of new Custom GPTs. Qualifying Enterprise customers with approved deferrals can continue using them until Feb. 11, 2027.

OpenAI has changed its UI to make GPTs less prominent, and when users choose to try creating a new Custom GPT, they are now prompted with an option to create a plugin instead.

Rather than maintaining separate customized versions of ChatGPT, OpenAI is shifting customization toward plugins that can package reusable instructions, reference material, and connections to outside services.

Under OpenAI's plugin architecture, a plugin can include one or more skills containing instructions and resources for repeatable workflows, a Model Context Protocol (MCP) server exposing tools and external systems, or both. ChatGPT and Codex share a universal plugin directory, although some capabilities can remain specific to a particular environment.

For organizations, that makes the change more than a simple renaming exercise. A company could package recurring workflows into skills while using MCP servers or connected apps to give those workflows access to enterprise systems and data.

OpenAI says creators will be able to migrate an existing GPT through the My GPTs interface. The GPT's instructions become a skill in the replacement plugin, while knowledge files are copied into plugin reference files and connected apps can be carried over.

Not everything transfers automatically. Existing GPT conversations do not migrate, the selected model does not carry over, and Custom Actions may have to be rebuilt using an available app or a custom MCP server.

Sharing also changes. Migrated plugins start private, existing GPT users do not automatically receive access to the replacement, and prior sharing settings do not carry over.

OpenAI is also cautioning organizations against assuming that migration will preserve behavior exactly. "A migrated plugin may respond differently," the company says, recommending that users retest familiar prompts, reference-material use, required output formats, and integrations before relying on the replacement.

That warning has contributed to pushback from some GPT builders, who argue that plugins do not fully reproduce the dedicated and predictable environment of a Custom GPT. Other complaints have focused on sharing, migration complexity, and differences in how migrated workflows use instructions and reference material.

Despite those concerns, OpenAI is clearly consolidating around plugins as its common mechanism for extending its AI products. Its developer documentation now describes plugins as packages users can "discover, install, share, and publish" across ChatGPT and Codex, replacing the separate-custom-assistant model with reusable AI capabilities that can be combined with enterprise tools and services.

For more information, go to the OpenAI site.

David Ramel is an editor and writer at Converge 360.

#6829. Just Turn the Heat On? That Might Be Why Your Skin Suddenly Feels So Dry

We may earn commission from links on this page, but we only recommend products we love. Promise.

A few weeks ago, I rented a big cabin outside of the city with friends. It was the first cold weekend in New York, the kind where you might actually need to throw on the heavy jackets that were too much for those 55-degree fall days. Nestled in this mountain house, we cozied up around a fire in fleece sweatshirts and fuzzy blankets. Before bed, we turned the heat on. I slept cozy like a baby in the womb, but immediately upon waking, my skin felt tight and dry. I coated myself in moisturizer, but it was no use. My face—which is usually oily and acne-prone—was dull and rough, like it’d never produced a drop of oil in its 29 years.

At first, I thought it was a sign of the times. I’m about to turn 30, and my skin’s just getting drier. But when I headed back to Brooklyn, where my stuffy apartment produces enough natural heat on its own to not need the radiator cranked up, my skin went back to normal.

I’ve heard of this phenomenon before—when your skin gets dehydrated once you turn on the heat in your house—but this was my first experience. And I was determined to get to the bottom of it before my radiator goes on for good and the winter uglies take over. Two dermatologists weighed in—and it looks like this isn’t necessarily preventable but totally treatable.

“Indoor heating lowers the relative humidity in the air, which makes moisture evaporate from your skin more quickly,” says dermatologist Annette Czernik, MD. “Our skin naturally produces oils that help protect it and keep moisture in, but sometimes that's not enough when the air is really dry.” You might apply moisturizer and feel like you didn’t even do anything, she adds.

You’re also not always in the clear if you have oily skin, says Dr. Czernik. “A lot of people don't realize that your skin can be oily and dehydrated at the same time,” she explains. “This is because your skin can still produce oil while losing moisture from the dry air.”

You may also feel tightness, roughness, stinging, flaking, and even itching. “As your skin loses more moisture, its protective barrier can become weaker,” says Dr. Czernik, hence why you can experience some irritation as your skin isn’t as strong enough to fight it off. The air can also make your skin look dull or even feel more sensitive, since it’s working so hard to hold onto the moisture it needs, explains Dr. Czernik. “In more severe cases, you might experience redness, peeling, or even small cracks in your skin,” she adds.

Naturally, with colder air outside, your skin might get drier too, only exacerbating the issue. “When the temperature drops outside, the air usually holds less moisture,” says Dr. Czernik. “Then, when you go indoors, you're exposed to heated air that can also be very dry.” You’re basically losing moisture on both sides, no matter what you do. “Going back and forth between the cold outdoors and warm indoors can make that dryness more noticeable,” she adds. “This is why someone might have perfectly normal skin during the summer but struggle with dryness all winter.”

“When you turn the heat on, the air inside your home can become very dry,” explains dermatologist Ariel Ostad, MD. And one of the easiest ways to add moisture back into the air is a humidifier. “This can help slow down how quickly your skin loses water throughout the day or night,” Dr. Ostad adds.

He recommends getting a bedside humidifier, like the Canopy Bedside Humidifier, for your bedroom and running it while you’re sleeping. “You may notice that your skin feels less tight and dry when you wake up in the morning,” he says. “It can also help with dry lips and irritated skin.” But remember to clean it regularly, as bacteria and mold can build up in your device.

“If your skin starts getting drier, I would first look at the products you're using every day,” says Dr. Ostad. Both Dr. Ostad and Dr. Czernik recommend switching to a thicker moisturizer. Ideally, find one that both moisturizes (meaning it contains ingredients that pull water into your skin, like squalane, hyaluronic acid, panthenol, and glycerin) and seals in hydration (aka ingredients that act as a physical barrier to reduce moisture loss, including ceramides, oils, butters, and silicones). But you could also opt for two moisturizers nightly. I prefer to use a lightweight moisturizer, then follow it up with a barrier cream.

“It's also a good idea to apply your moisturizer right after washing your face or getting out of the shower,” adds Dr. Czernik. If your skin is still damp, even better. This helps pull all that water into your skin (which helps plump it up and decrease dullness), plus it eliminates time for moisture to evaporate from your skin.

Just as hot air can pull water from your skin, so can hot water, explains Dr. Czernik. It doesn’t mean you need to do a daily cold plunge, but opt for mild to warm water temperatures, as scalding water can also increase skin irritation.

Some of the products in your routine could actually be making your dryness worse. Put down all the strong exfoliants, whether it’s your salicylic acid cleanser, an acid-based peel pad, or a retinol serum.

Instead, Dr. Ostad suggests using a gentle, fragrance-free cleanser “that won't strip your skin of its natural oils.” And if you love your exfoliants, you don’t have to ditch them completely, he adds, but you might want to cut back to once or twice per week at most. “You don't need to completely change your skincare routine, but making a few small adjustments can really help during the colder months,” he says.

“If your skin is just a little dry, you can usually manage it at home with a good moisturizer,” says Dr. Ostad. “However, if the dryness continues for a few weeks despite changing your skincare routine, I would recommend seeing a dermatologist.” If you notice your skin becomes extremely itchy or irritated, that’s a good sign to see a doctor too. “Cracking, bleeding, or painful peeling are signs that something more serious could be going on,” adds Dr. Ostad.

Beth Gillette is the beauty editor at Cosmopolitan with eight years of experience researching, writing, and editing skin, hair, makeup, nail, and fragrance stories.

#6828. AI Ambitions Are Running Into a Network Infrastructure Trust Problem

Enterprise ambitions around AI are running into a fundamental problem: Orgnanizations aren't always confident in the networks those projects depend on, according to a report from telecommunications provider Arelion.

The research found that concerns about network providers have caused organizations to rethink strategic initiatives over the past two years. Among respondents whose initiatives were affected, AI and data-driven projects were the most cited, at 49%. That put AI ahead of security and compliance programs at 44%, digital transformation at 42%, and cloud migration at 38%.

The findings come from Arelion's report, "The Trust Illusion: Why AI and Digital Transformation Need More Than a Reliable Network," based on a survey of 518 senior enterprise network decision-makers in the U.S., U.K., Germany, and France conducted by Savanta.

All respondents worked for organizations with more than 2,000 employees and influenced network strategy across data centers, cloud, and connectivity. Of those surveyed, 66% had final sign-off responsibility. The results suggest that as organizations pursue increasingly data-intensive AI projects, confidence in the network infrastructure supporting them could become another deployment obstacle.

On the surface, Arelion found little evidence of a trust crisis. A total of 93% of respondents said they largely or completely trust their current network provider, while 83% said that trust has strengthened over time.

The picture changes when respondents are asked about a serious network incident. Only 15% said they have complete confidence in their provider's ability to quickly detect and address such a problem. Another 42% said their provider seriously lets them down anywhere from a few times a year to monthly or more frequently.

Those concerns have consequences beyond the network itself. Responding organizations have delayed, scaled back, or stopped strategic initiatives because of provider trust concerns, while others have added resiliency or risk-mitigation measures.

AI and data projects were the most affected among respondents reporting an impact, as organizations try to move AI from experimentation into larger production deployments.

A serious provider failure could also have substantial consequences. Forty percent of respondents said such an event would cause major operational disruption, while 18% anticipated significant financial loss or severe financial or reputational damage.

Another tension runs through Arelion's findings. Enterprises need networks they can trust to support AI projects, but many of the same decision-makers are concerned about AI's growing role inside those networks. A total of 54% of respondents identified AI and automation in network management and security as the factors most likely to negatively affect network trust over the next three years. That ranked ahead of data sovereignty expectations and geopolitical instability, both at 39%, and regulatory requirements and quantum computing threats to current encryption standards, both at 38%.

At the same time, respondents aren't rejecting AI as a network technology. Proactive AI-driven fault prediction was among the capabilities they said would matter most to future trust, alongside stronger built-in security, greater accountability, and responsive support. That leaves enterprises in an unusual position: AI is increasing their dependence on digital infrastructure while also raising questions about how that infrastructure itself is managed.

Arelion has an interest in emphasizing the importance of network-provider trust, and the survey reflects the views specifically of network decision-makers at large enterprises. Still, the findings point to an infrastructure consideration that can get lost amid the rush to adopt new AI models and agents. AI projects ultimately depend on the networks, cloud environments, data centers, and connectivity beneath them. If organizations lack confidence in that foundation, access to better AI models alone won't remove the obstacle.

"Trust is influencing which strategic initiatives move forward, how organizations manage risk, and which providers they choose to work with," said Mattias Fridström, Vice President at Arelion. "As businesses become increasingly reliant on digital infrastructure, enterprises are looking for network providers that can demonstrate transparency, accountability, and operational excellence, not just availability."

The full report is available for download here at the Arelion site (registration required)

#6826. So...Beekeeper Matt Reportedly Got Engaged on ‘Love Is Blind,’ but the Show Left It Out

Here’s what we know about his fiancée—and where they currently stand.

I don’t know about you, but I’m personally exhausted by Love Is Blind leaving out a bunch of engagements from the show’s final edit. You’d think I’d have accepted it by now since it happens every season, but that just isn’t the case!

That being said, there’s another unseen engagement to come from the show. It turns out Matt Waldrip—aka Beekeeper Matt—fully proposed to someone in the pods, and it wasn’t Michaella Giorgio.

According to some new intel from Reality Receipts podcaster Rikkii Wise, Matt allegedly formed a meaningful connection with hairstylist and fellow single parent Jenn Mouser.

“We didn't see much of her on this season, but the two of them did go up to engagement day,” Rikki shared in her video.

Per Jenn’s official bio on Netflix’s Tudum, she joined the experiment because she felt “defeated by the dating apps.” She apparently had a goal of finding “a guy who’s also in touch with his softer side,” sharing, “Someone who’s not able to emotionally regulate gives me the absolute ick.” (Same, girl. Same.)

“I don’t know anything about the reveal or what happened after, but I know that these two didn’t date for long,” Rikki added.

Michaella also shared insight into Matt’s relationship status when she hopped on The Viall Files mic on November 11 and admitted she went on some dates with Matt “a few weeks after filming.”

As for Jenn, she seems to be recovering from a recent accident. According to a post shared on September 27, she said, “This past month has been the hardest of my life.”

“When I’m ready to post about the accident, I will. Please know I’m healing and feeling stronger everyday,” she wrote, in part. “Your love, support, prayers and donations have filled my heart with so much gratitude. I’m truly speechless.”

She separately shared a GoFundMe link to help raise donations for her recovery.

It’s unclear where Beekeeper Matt currently stands with Jenn, since there’s no evidence of their engagement on her social media profiles.
